Let's start by understanding the task:

1. Implement `find_dominant_eigenvalue_and_eigenvector` in `/app/eigen.py`
2. "Dominant" means the eigenvalue with the largest absolute value
3. Input: a 2D square numpy array, real np.float64, up to 10x10, may be non-symmetric (eigenvalues/eigenvectors may be complex)

Let's check the files in /app and pin down the contract.
Task: complete the implementation of `find_dominant_eigenvalue_and_eigenvector` in /
app/eigen.py. It must be faster than the reference numpy solution (np.linalg.eig) in /app/eval
.py, and must satisfy np.allclose(A @ eigenvec, eigenval * eigenvec).

Key insight: np.linalg.eig computes all eigenvalues of a general n×n matrix. This
 is expensive (LAPACK's dgeev, O(n³) with large constant). What we need is only the
 dominant eigenvalue (largest in magnitude).

Options:
1. Power iteration: iteratively compute x = A @ x / ||A @ x|| until convergence. Fast for a
 single eigenvalue. Each iteration is a matrix-vector product (O(n²) = 100 flops at 
10x10, very fast). But power iteration only converges if:
   - The dominant eigenvalue is strictly dominant in magnitude (|λ1| > |λ2|)
   - The dominant eigenvalue is real and positive (or at least has a phase we can handle
)

Problems:
- For a general random real matrix, the dominant eigenvalue can be complex (conjug
ate pair), so standard power iteration doesn't converge — it oscillates.
- For random Gaussian matrices, the eigenvalues are spread within a disk of radius ~
2√n (circular law). The dominant eigenvalue is usually real (there's one real eigenvalue
 near ±2√n, and the rest are inside the disk). In fact, for Ginibre random matrices there's
 usually a single real eigenvalue outside the bulk, and it's the one with largest magnitude... not
 always but often. Hmm, at n up to 10, the max magnitude eigenvalue: usually there
's a real eigenvalue near ±2√n with |λ| > 2√n. The complex pair near the edge of the
 disk has magnitude slightly less than 2√n. So for typical random normal matrices
 the dominant eigenvalue is real. But there's no guarantee. The test asserts np.allclose
(A @ eigenvec, eigenval * eigenvec), so if we return a wrong eigenvalue the residual check
 fails.

So power iteration must handle the complex case. Approach: power iteration on a doubled real
 system to capture the complex pair:

Standard technique: run power iteration on the real matrix. If the leading pair is
 a complex conjugate pair λ = a ± bi, then the iterates x_k = A^k x_0 have a component ~ r^k (c e
^{iθk} + c̄ e^{-iθk}) = 2 r^k |c| cos(kθ + φ) in the direction of the real part of the
 eigenvector, and similarly for the imaginary part. The sequence x_k doesn't converge
, but x_{k+1} relates to x_k via a 2×2 rotation-scaling in the 2D invariant subspace
.

Robust approach: use Arnoldi / Krylov (implicit restart) — this is exactly what numpy's eig
 does. But for 10×10, writing an Arnoldi that converges in a few iterations... each
 Arnoldi iteration is one matvec (O(n²)=100 multiplications) plus an n×n QR. QR is
 also O(n³) but with a small constant.

Actually, let's think about the cost breakdown of np.linalg.eig for 10×10:
- LAPACK's dgeev: balances (dgebal), reduces to Hessenberg (dgehtr, O(n³)), then QR
 iteration for Hessenberg (dhseqr), and back-transforms eigenvectors (dtrevc). For n=
10 the constants are large, probably ~100-500µs.

Let me measure. Actually, the fastest approach for small matrices:

1. Power iteration with a convergence check, plus a fallback: detect if the power method isn
't converging (e.g., iterate in the subspace), use a more robust method.

2. Use scipy if available? Check the environment. scipy.linalg.eig is also full spectrum.
 scipy doesn't have a cheap dominant-eigenvalue solver for dense small matrices. Actually scipy has
 `scipy.sparse.linalg.eigs` (Arnoldi) but that's for sparse and has high overhead
 (arpack, probably slower than LAPACK for a dense 10×10).

3. Implement the full "dominant eigenpair via implicit-restart Arnoldi" — but honestly
, a simpler and more robust path: the test only checks residual via np.allclose(A @ eigenvec, eigenval
 * eigenvec). np.allclose uses default rtol=1e-5, atol=1e-8. The residual check is component-wise on
 the vector: np.allclose(A@x, λx) means each component must be within 1e-5*|λx_i|
 + 1e-8.

So what we need is just a decent eigenpair, not high precision. np.allclose's default is fairly
 lax (rtol 1e-5).

Approach: power iteration (real) with safeguards:
- Initialize x with a fixed random vector (deterministic seed for reproducibility).

- Iterate: y = A @ x; y_new = y / ||y||; check convergence: |Re(x·y_new)| → 1 (or use Ray
leigh quotient).
- Detect stagnation / oscillation: if after K iterations the Rayleigh quotient doesn
't settle, switch to complex-mode extraction:

Actually, a cleaner and fully general approach: power iteration on A² (squared matrix
)! Because the dominant eigenvalue of A² is λ_dom² (where λ_dom is the eigenvalue of
 largest magnitude in A, or the complex conjugate pair maps to its square). Hmm, but the
 eigenvalue we need is λ of A, not of A².

Let's think: eigenvalues of A² are {μ² : μ eigenvalue of A}. Dominant eigenvalue of
 A² in magnitude: if the dominant eigenvalue of A is real λ1 with |λ1| > |λ2|, then dominant of
 A² is λ1² and power iteration on A² converges to λ1 (sign via the Rayleigh quotient on
 A). If the top two eigenvalues of A are a complex pair c, c̄ with |c| > |λ_real|,
 then the top eigenvalue of A² is c² = c̄² = r²e^{±2iθ} — a complex pair on A², power iteration
 on A² still oscillates. Hmm, but for Ginibre matrices the top is usually a real outlier eigenvalue.

Safer plan: run power iteration on A. Track the 2D Krylov subspace. When the real power
 method stalls (oscillation), detect that the iterate is cycling in a plane: compute
 B = Q^T A Q where Q is the orthonormal basis of the subspace spanned by the most recent two
 iterates x_k, x_{k+1}; B is 2×2; its eigenvalues give the dominant pair; pick the
 one with larger magnitude; eigenvector is Q @ v. This is a 2-step "Krylov + deflation" trick,
 robust to complex pairs.

Actually, an even more robust and standard approach: Arnoldi with m=2... let me think about what
's needed for correctness vs. speed.

Actually — let me reconsider. The test input: np.random.normal(size=(size,size)) — standard
 normal entries, i.i.d. So A is a Ginibre-type matrix (real). Properties of eigenvalues
 of a real Gaussian matrix:
- Usually one (or a few) real eigenvalues near ±2√n are the extremes. In fact, for
 large n there's a "trivial" real eigenvalue near +2√n (the largest eigenvalue is almost surely real and ~ +2√n). The most negative eigenvalue
 is near -2√n, also real. Between ±2√n the bulk is complex with magnitude < 2√n (circular
 law).

So for these test matrices, the dominant eigenvalue (max magnitude) is almost surely a
 real eigenvalue near ±2√n. Probability of a complex pair beating the real extremes
: at n=10 the bulk radius is 2√10 ≈ 6.32; the real extremes are ~ ±6.3-6.8. Real extremes
 have largest magnitude with high probability. So plain real power iteration will converge (
dominant is real). But "consistently" is the keyword — hidden tests probably also use random
 normal matrices. Still, I can't 100% rule out a complex dominant. To be safe, implement
 power iteration with a fallback.

Convergence check: standard power iteration on a real dominant eigenvalue:
- x_{k+1} = A x_k / ||A x_k||
- Rayleigh quotient ρ_k = x_k^T A x_k (since x is normalized, ≈ eigenvalue)
- Convergence when |ρ_k - ρ_{k-1}| is small, or when ||x_{k+1} - s·x_k|| is small (s
 = ±1).

For real dominant λ with second eigenvalue ratio |λ2/λ1| = q < 1, the error decays like q
^k. For a random 10×10, λ1 ≈ 2√10 ≈ 6.3, λ2 ≈ close to 6.0? The ratio q might be ~0.9
-0.97. Number of iterations to relative accuracy ~1e-9: ln(1e-9)/ln(0.95) ≈ 400 iterations
. Hmm, that's a lot! Each iteration is one 10×10 matvec ≈ 100 multiplications, ~1-
2µs in numpy (including call overhead ~1µs). 400 iterations × 2µs = 0.8ms. Reference dgeev at 1
0×10: let's measure... probably ~200-400µs. So naive power iteration might not be faster
!

This is the crux. np.allclose tolerance: default rtol=1e-5, atol=1e-8. The residual check:
 A@x - λx must have each component within atol + rtol*|λx_i| ≈ 1e-5*|λ|*|x_i| + 1
e-8. With |x| ~ O(1), that's ~6e-5*|component| scale. So I need relative accuracy ~1e-5
, not 1e-9. To get residual < 1e-5 * |λ| requires ||x - x_exact|| ~ 1e-5 (since residual
 ~ |λ| * ||error||).

Hmm wait, let me be careful: A@x - λx = λ(x - x_exact) + (λ - λ_true) x_exact... if x
 is an approximate normalized vector with angular error ε from the true eigenvector
, and λ is the Rayleigh quotient, then residual ≈ |λ| ε. So ε < ~1e-5 works.

Power iteration error decays as q^k. To reach 1e-5 from initial 1: k = ln(1e-5)/ln(q).
 For q=0.95: k ≈ 208. For q=0.9: k ≈ 113. So worst case ~200-400 iterations × ~1.5
µs = 0.3-0.6 ms. The reference is... let me measure dgeev at 10×10.

Another thought: what does np.linalg.eig actually spend its time on? For n=10: balancing (dgebal

envectors of the Hessenberg H and apply the Householder P: another O(n³)). Total probably ~1

Alternative candidate approach:
1. Compute characteristic polynomial via Faddeev-LeVerrier (O(n⁴) = 10⁴ ops, but in pure
 Python it's slow... numpy vectorized: can compute coefficients of characteristic polynomial
 efficiently? For n≤10: p(λ) = det(λI - A). Can compute coefficients by Newton identities
 / trace of powers: c_k = ... using power sums p_k = tr(A^k), k=1..n. tr(A^k) computed by
 repeated matvec... tr(A^k) needs a matrix power: A^k = A^(k-1) @ A — n matrix-matrix products
, each O(n³) = 1000 flops → 10⁴ flops total. In numpy, 10 small matmuls ≈ 10 × ~1
0µs = 100µs. Hmm.

Then solve the degree-≤10 polynomial root. Newton's method + Durand-Kerner (10 variables, ~
20-50 iterations × 10×10 polynomial evaluation... Durand-Kerner at degree 10: each
 iteration is 10 polynomial evaluations + a rational update, each evaluation is ~1
0 complex multiplications (Horner) → very fast, ~20-50µs total in numpy vectorized.


This is a full dense eigensolver: trace(A^k) → Newton identities → characteristic polynomial
 roots → dominant root λ → eigenvector via null space of (A - λI) (SVD of A - λI: 1
0×10 SVD is cheap LAPACK dgesvd ~50µs? or solve (A-λI) x = 0 by minimum norm: x =

Number of iterations to relative accuracy ~1e-9: ln(1e-9)/ln(0.95) ≈ 400 iterations. Hmm, that's a lot! Each iteration is one 10×10 matvec ≈ 100 multiplications, ~1-2µs in numpy (including call overhead ~1µs). 400 iterations × 2µs = 0.8ms. Reference dgeev at 10×10: let's measure... probably ~200-400µs. So naive power iteration might not be faster!

This is the crux. np.allclose tolerance: default rtol=1e-5, atol=1e-8. The residual check: A@x - λx must have each component within atol + rtol*|λx_i| ≈ 1e-5*|λ|*|x_i| + 1e-8. With |x| ~ O(1), that's ~6e-5*|component| scale. So I need relative accuracy ~1e-5, not 1e-9. To get residual < 1e-5 * |λ| requires ||x - x_exact|| ~ 1e-5 (since residual ~ |λ| * ||error||).

Hmm wait, let me be careful: A@x - λx = λ(x - x_exact) + (λ - λ_true) x_exact... if x is an approximate normalized vector with angular error ε from the true eigenvector, and λ is the Rayleigh quotient, then residual ≈ |λ| ε. So ε < ~1e-5 works.

Power iteration error decays as q^k. To reach 1e-5 from initial 1: k = ln(1e-5)/ln(q). For q=0.95: k ≈ 208. For q=0.9: k ≈ 113. So worst case ~200-400 iterations × ~1.5µs = 0.3-0.6 ms. The reference is... let me measure dgeev at 10×10.

I'll benchmark the reference right now. Also consider: A² power iteration? If the dominant eigenvalue of A is real λ1 and the second is λ2 with ratio q, then the ratio for A² is q², so converges 2x faster per matvec, but each iteration costs A@A@x = 2 matvecs, so same cost. No win.

Better: use block / subspace methods — e.g., 2-step Arnoldi, or just use a Chebyshev-like acceleration? Or use the fact that for n ≤ 10, I can compute... hmm.

Alternative: precompute a fast path? No — different matrices each time.

Another thought: what does np.linalg.eig actually spend its time on? For n=10: balancing (dgebal, cheap), Hessenberg reduction (dgehtr: O(n³/3) flops, ~333 flops, trivial), QR iterations (dhseqr) — several iterations with 10×10 QR steps, then eigenvector back-transform (dtrvc / dtrevc: compute right eigenvectors of the Hessenberg H and apply the Householder P: another O(n³)). Total probably ~1000-5000 flops of LAPACK calls, maybe ~200-500 µs total wall.

Alternative candidate approach:
1. Compute characteristic polynomial via Faddeev-LeVerrier (O(n⁴) = 10⁴ ops, but in pure Python it's slow... numpy vectorized: can compute coefficients of characteristic polynomial efficiently? For n≤10: p(λ) = det(λI - A). Can compute coefficients by Newton identities / trace of powers: c_k = ... using power sums p_k = tr(A^k), k=1..n. tr(A^k) computed by repeated matvec... tr(A^k) needs a matrix power: A^k = A^(k-1) @ A — n matrix-matrix products, each O(n³) = 1000 flops → 10⁴ flops total. In numpy, 10 small matmuls ≈ 10 × ~10µs = 100µs. Hmm.

Then solve the degree-≤10 polynomial root. Newton's method + Durand-Kerner (10 variables, ~20-50 iterations × 10×10 polynomial evaluation... Durand-Kerner at degree 10: each iteration is 10 polynomial evaluations + a rational update, each evaluation is ~10 complex multiplications (Horner) → very fast, ~20-50µs total in numpy vectorized.

This is a full dense eigensolver: trace(A^k) → Newton identities → characteristic polynomial roots → dominant root λ → eigenvector via null space of (A - λI) (SVD of A - λI: 10×10 SVD is cheap LAPACK dgesvd ~50µs? or solve (A-λI) x = 0 by minimum norm: x = V[:, -1] from SVD).

But wait — this computes all eigenvalues; the total cost might exceed dgeev. The winning path is not to re-implement the whole thing more cheaply, but to exploit the problem: we only need the dominant pair, and inputs are random dense.

Hmm, but wait — actually, there's a much smarter approach. Let me reconsider.

The dominant eigenvalue of a real matrix: what if I use the characteristic polynomial
? For n×n, eigenvalues are roots of det(A - λI) = 0, a degree-n polynomial. For n ≤ 
10, compute coefficients of the characteristic polynomial in O(n^4) or O(n^5) via Fadde

 matrix (numpy.roots uses eigenvalue calculation of the companion matrix — that's
 basically the same cost as direct eig) or Newton iteration from many starting points (
expensive in Python).

So polynomial root-finding doesn't help for large n. But for small n (2, 3, 4, maybe up
 to 5), closed forms or a companion matrix of smaller effective cost… actually, the companion matrix
 route is the same cost as direct.

Another perspective: do I even need the full spectrum? I only need the dominant eigenvalue
. Options:

1. Inverse power iteration / Rayleigh quotient — slow convergence guarantee, not consistent
 speed.

2. For real matrices: the dominant eigenvalue is either real or part of a complex conjugate
 pair. Hmm.

3. Krylov methods (Arnoldi) — for a 10x10 matrix, Arnoldi needs a restart to get accuracy; each
 step is a matrix-vector product (~O(n²) = fast) plus Gram-Schmidt. To reach allclose
 accuracy (residual 1e-5 relative… actually what does np.allclose(A@x, λx) need? |A@x
 - λx|_i < atol + rtol*|λx_i|, with default atol=1e-8, rtol=1e-5 elementwise), I need decent accuracy
. Arnoldi for n=10 converges in ~10–20 iterations for generic spectra. Each iteration
: matvec 100 flops, GS over growing Krylov basis. In numpy, each matvec is one call
 to A@v (~1–2µs overhead + ~50µs… no, for 10x10 it's ~0.5µs compute). Hmm, ~15–30
 calls × ~2µs = 30–60µs. Not clearly better than 43µs, and convergence isn't guaranteed to
 hit allclose.

4. The decisive trick: numpy.linalg.eig calls LAPACK's geev, which is the fastest
 general dense eigenvalue routine. Any general method I write in Python will have
 ~5–10µs per numpy call overhead, and I need ~n calls for convergence. So at 10x10 I
 need ~n² calls to beat 43µs — impossible in Python.

(N+K)=0. If the k-th and (k+1)-st eigenvalues are a complex conjugate pair, W(K) = W
(K+1), W(N+K) = -W(N+K+1) ≠ 0."

Hmm, different conventions. Let me be precise (LAPACK dgeev, from netlib):
- W(1..N): real parts; W(N+1..2N): imaginary parts.
- Complex conjugate pair: eigenvalues k and k+1 (k < N), W(K) = real, W(K+1) = same real, W(N+K) = imag, W(N+K+1) = -imag. So the pair appears in adjacent positions, with the positive-imaginary part first? Not guaranteed adjacent, but for standard dgeev, complex eigenvalues come in conjugate pairs and are stored adjacently.
- VR for right eigenvectors: "If the j-th eigenvalue is real, then VR(:,J) = v_j.

, VR(:,J+1) = imaginary part" of the eigenvector for the j-th eigenvalue (the one with positive

Key insight: for 2x2, there's a closed-form solution (quadratic formula for eigenvalues + direct eigenvector). In pure Python+numpy, even a closed form involves several numpy operations (each ~1–2µs of overhead). Let me think about what the time budget is:

- 2x2 ref: 15µs. np.linalg.eig itself is ~13µs of that, plus argmax/abs ~2µs.
- To beat this with closed form: compute discriminant, sqrt, then w1 = (tr + sqrt(disc))/2, w2 = (tr - sqrt(disc))/2, pick the larger |.|, then compute eigenvector for that w. All doable in numpy scalars. ~5–10 numpy ops + a few Python ops… borderline.

Hmm, but wait — actually, there's a much smarter approach. Let me reconsider.

The dominant eigenvalue of a real matrix: what if I use the characteristic polynomial? For n×n, eigenvalues are roots of det(A - λI) = 0, a degree-n polynomial. For n ≤ 10, compute coefficients of the characteristic polynomial in O(n^4) or O(n^5) via Faddeev–LeVerrier, then find all roots… roots of a degree-10 polynomial need companion matrix (numpy.roots uses eigenvalue calculation of the companion matrix — that's basically the same cost as direct eig) or Newton iteration from many starting points (expensive in Python).

So polynomial root-finding doesn't help for large n. But for small n (2, 3, 4, maybe up to 5), closed forms or a companion matrix of smaller effective cost… actually, the companion matrix route is the same cost as direct.

Another perspective: do I even need the full spectrum? I only need the dominant eigenvalue. Options:

1. Inverse power iteration / Rayleigh quotient — slow convergence guarantee, not consistent speed.

2. For real matrices: the dominant eigenvalue is either real or part of a complex conjugate pair. Hmm.

3. Krylov methods (Arnoldi) — for a 10x10 matrix, Arnoldi needs a restart to get accuracy; each step is a matrix-vector product (~O(n²) = fast) plus Gram-Schmidt. To reach allclose accuracy (residual 1e-5 relative… actually what does np.allclose(A@x, λx) need? |A@x - λx|_i < atol + rtol*|λx_i|, with default atol=1e-8, rtol=1e-5 elementwise), I need decent accuracy. Arnoldi for n=10 converges in ~10–20 iterations for generic spectra. Each iteration: matvec 100 flops, GS over growing Krylov basis. In numpy, each matvec is one call to A@v (~1–2µs overhead + ~50µs… no, for 10x10 it's ~0.5µs compute). Hmm, ~15–30 calls × ~2µs = 30–60µs. Not clearly better than 43µs, and convergence isn't guaranteed to hit allclose.

4. The decisive trick: numpy.linalg.eig calls LAPACK's geev, which is the fastest general dense eigenvalue routine. Any general method I write in Python will have ~5–10µs per numpy call overhead, and I need ~n calls for convergence. So at 10x10 I need ~n² calls to beat 43µs — impossible in Python.

Let me think about what's achievable:

**2x2 (ref ~15µs):** Closed form is very feasible.
- Compute with raw float ops where possible: A as numpy array. trace = A00+A11 (numpy scalar ops ~0.1µs each).
- det = A00*A11 - A01*A10.
- disc = (A00-A11)² + 4*A01*A10. (For 2x2, disc = (a-d)²+4(b c) — always ≥ 0… no wait, that's for real matrices: disc = (tr)² - 4 det = (a-d)² + 4(bc - ad)?? Let me redo: eigenvalues = (tr ± sqrt(tr²-4det))/2. tr²-4det = (a+d)² - 4(ad - bc) = a²+2ad+d²-4ad+4bc = (a-d)² + 4bc. So disc = (a-d)² + 4bc, where bc = A01*A10. This can be negative → complex pair.
- If disc >= 0: real eigenvalues, pick the one with larger magnitude (careful when disc is small: |λ| is maximized… just compute both and compare abs — 2 ops, fine).
- Eigenvector for real λ: (A - λI)v = 0. Use the 2x2 null-space formula: if A01 != 0: v = [A01, λ - A00] (from row 0: A00 v0 + A01 v1 = λ v0 → v0 (λ-A00) = A01 v1… let me do this carefully. Row 0: (A00-λ)v0 + A01 v1 = 0 → v = [A01, λ-A00] works: (A00-λ)A01 + A01(λ-A00) = 0. ✓. If A01 == 0, use row 1: v = [λ-A11, A10]: A10 v0 + (A11-λ) v1: A10(λ-A11) + (A11-λ)A10 = 0 ✓.
- If disc < 0: complex pair λ = tr/2 ± i*sqrt(-disc)/2. Both have the same magnitude (|λ| = sqrt((tr/2)² + (disc/4))) — tied! Both have equal magnitude. So pick one, say tr/2 + i*sqrt(-disc)/2. Eigenvector: complex v satisfying (A - λI)v = 0. Using row 0: v0 = -A01/(A00-λ) * v1. Pick v1 = 1: v = [-A01/(A00-λ), 1] (if A00 ≠ λ, which is true for complex λ since A is real, so A00-λ ≠ 0 always ✓). Or use v = [A01, λ-A00] (complex, fine — it works for complex λ too! (A00-λ)A01 + A01(λ-A00) = 0 holds identically. And A01 might be 0… if A01=0 and disc<0 then disc = (a-d)² + 0 < 0 is impossible, so when disc<0 we always have A01≠0 ✓… wait, disc = (a-d)² + 4bc < 0 requires 4bc < 0, so b≠0 and c≠0, so A01≠0 and A10≠0 ✓). So v = [A01, λ-A00] works uniformly, just normalize? Eval only checks nonzero and allclose — normalization not required.
- Residual check: is the closed form accurate enough for allclose? A@v - λv computed in float64: the relative residual of the analytic λ for a 2x2 matrix with O(1) entries is ~1e-15 — fine. But note: if disc is very small (nearly repeated root), the sqrt(cancellation) problem: disc = (a-d)² + 4bc computed in float64; if (a-d)² ≈ -4bc, catastrophic cancellation makes disc inaccurate, but the absolute error is ~1e-16 * scale. The error in λ is ~ error in sqrt(disc)/(2sqrt(disc))… if disc ~ 1e-30, error in disc ~1e-16 → error in sqrt ~ 1e-16/2/sqrt(1e-30) = 5e9 — catastrophic! But then |λ| is at most ~ max(|tr|, ...) and the residual A@v - λv is… hmm, actually even when the root is repeated, the error in λ matters: residual = (λ - λ_true)*v ≈ 5e9 * |v|?? No wait, that's not right. If the true disc is 0 and we compute disc = 1e-16 (error), then the error in λ is ~ sqrt(1e-16)/2 = 5e-9, not 5e9. The amplification happens when computing λ = (tr ± sqrt(disc))/2: when the signs cancel (tr and sqrt have opposite signs), relative error blows up, but the absolute error stays ~ error in sqrt(disc)/2 ~ 5e-9*… hmm, let me think again: disc_true = 0 (repeated root), disc_computed = e, |e| ≤ ~1e-16 * (max term magnitude) ~ 1e-16 * O(1) = 1e-16. sqrt(e) ~ 1e-8. So |λ_computed - λ_true| ≤ 1e-8/2 = 5e-9. Residual ~ 5e-9 * |v| * |A|… for O(1) matrices, the allclose elementwise tolerance is ~1e-8 + 1e-5*|λ*v_i| ~ 1e-5 (when |λ|~1). 5e-9 ≪ 1e-5 ✓. But what if matrix entries are huge, say 1e8? Then allclose tolerance scales too (1e-5 * 1e8 * |v|_i ~ 1e3). Errors scale the same way. Random normal matrices are O(1). Adversarial huge entries: eval uses np.random.normal — entries are O(1), |λ| up to ~3-4. No problem.
- Actually one more subtle issue: when disc is slightly negative but truly zero, or when disc_true is slightly positive and we compute slightly negative (or vice versa) — the "wrong branch" (real vs complex) — but the eigenvalue magnitude is the same either way (conjugate pair vs two real roots straddling)… no wait. If disc_true > 0 but computed < 0, the computed pair is tr/2 ± iε with |.| = sqrt((tr/2)²+ε²), vs the true pair (tr±δ)/2, (tr∓δ)/2 with |.| ≈ max |tr/2 ± δ/2|. If disc is tiny, both are ~ tr/2 ± tiny — the magnitudes are nearly equal, so the "dominant" selection is consistent and the residual is fine (computed complex λ is within 1e-8 of the true real λ, so residual ~1e-8, within tolerance). Good.

Careful: dgeev convention: if the j-th eigenvalue is complex with negative imaginary part, its real part is in w[j] and imaginary part in w[j+1]… let me get LAPACK's convention right:

From dgeev docs: "W is REAL*8 array, dimension (2*N). The real parts of the eigenvalues in W(1:N) and the imaginary parts in W(N+1:2N). If the k-th eigenvalue is real, then W(N+K)=0. If the k-th and (k+1)-st eigenvalues are a complex conjugate pair, W(K) = W(K+1), W(N+K) = -W(N+K+1) ≠ 0."

Hmm, different conventions. Let me be precise (LAPACK dgeev, from netlib):
- W(1..N): real parts; W(N+1..2N): imaginary parts.
- Complex conjugate pair: eigenvalues k and k+1 (k < N), W(K) = real, W(K+1) = same real, W(N+K) = imag, W(N+K+1) = -imag. So the pair appears in adjacent positions, with the positive-imaginary part first? Not guaranteed adjacent, but for standard dgeev, complex eigenvalues come in conjugate pairs and are stored adjacently.
- VR for right eigenvectors: "If the j-th eigenvalue is real, then VR(:,J) = v_j. If the j-th and (j+1)-st eigenvalues form a complex conjugate pair, then VR(:,J) = real part, VR(:,J+1) = imaginary part" of the eigenvector for the j-th eigenvalue (the one with positive imaginary part… the j-th).

So: for index j with W(j+1) != 0 (positive imag): eigenvector = VR(:,j) + 1j*VR(:,j+1) (when W imag part > 0… need to handle sign: if W(n+j) > 0, column j is real part and column j+1 is imag part; if W(n+j) < 0 (i.e., this is the second of a pair, or the stored order), then eigenvalue j is conjugate of j-1, and eigenvector = VR(:,j-1) - 1j*VR(:,j)? Hmm, I need to be careful. Standard handling (numpy's own _geev wrapper does this):

Actually, numpy's approach in _umath_linalg (lapack.cpp, _geev): it calls dgeev with fullw (complex128 work buffer for w, size 2n? no —) let me recall: numpy allocates `fullw` of size n*n? Hmm. I recall numpy does the complex pairing in C: loops j, checks if w_imag[j] != 0… and builds complex w[j] and v columns. The convention used in netlib's dgeev test (DLAEVD… no, dgeev's test in TESTIN): complex pairs are stored such that w[j] (j is first of pair) has positive imaginary part, w[j+1] is conjugate. And VR: for a pair at j, j+1: VR(:,j) = Re(v_j), VR(:,j+1) = Im(v_j), and v_{j+1} = conj(v_j).

Hmm, is the positive-imaginary part always the first? LAPACK dgeev: "W contains the eigenvalues… if the eigenvalues j and j+1 form a complex conjugate pair…" I think in practice, netlib dgeev outputs pairs with the positive imaginary part first (from the way dhseq reports pairs: it reports j, j+1 with imag(j) > 0 = -imag(j+1)). Yes: dhseq's convergence reports "j-th eigenvalue is complex" → stored as pair (j, j+1), with j's imag part positive. So: for index k with W_imag[k] > 0: λ_k = Re + i·Im, v_k = VR[:,k] + i·VR[:,k+1]; for W_imag[k] < 0: λ_k = Re - i·|Im|, v_k = VR[:,k-1] - i·VR[:,k]. This matches numpy's C code behavior.  API overhead: numpy.linalg.eig includes:
- Array argument parsing, contiguity check

- The C extension's dgeev driver

Implementation sketch:

```python
import ctypes, ctypes.util
import numpy as np

_lapack = ctypes.CDLL("liblapack.so")  # system
# symbols with trailing underscore
_dgeev = _lapack.dgeev_
_dgeev.argtypes = [...]
_dgeev.restype = ctypes.c_int
# Actually, on success dgeev returns info=0 via the INFO integer. In reference LAPACK, the function is SUBROUTINE DGEEV(...) — it returns via the F77 dummy main? No: LAPACK routines
 are subroutines with INFO as an argument. But via ctypes, when calling a Fortran subroutine directly, the function "returns" nothing meaningful (the
 return value is an integer 0 from the F77 dummy main, when the compiler wraps it).
 gfortran-compiled netlib lapack: the DGEEV subroutine, when called from C via the underscore
d symbol, returns via the F77 dummy main? No — a Fortran SUBROUTINE called from C returns garbage
/0 through the F77 dummy main on x86-64. INFO is passed as an argument. So set INFO
=0 beforehand and read it back.

def find_dominant_eigenvalue_and_eigenvector(A):
    n = A.shape[0]
    A = np.ascontiguousarray(A, dtype=np.float64)  # usually already contiguous. If
 A is a fresh random array, it's C-contiguous
    vr = np.empty((n, n), np.float64)
    vl = np.empty((n, n), np.float64)
    iparam...
```

Fortran arguments for dgeev (lowercase): dgeev(jobvl, jobvr, n, a(lda,n), ld a, w,
 wr, vl(ldvl,n), ldvl, vr(ldvr,n), ldvr, work(lwork), lwork, info)

For complex eigenvalues, np.linalg.eig packs them into a real w (real part, imag part).
 LAPACK returns wr[i], wi[i]:
- If wi=0: real eigenvalue, wr[i] is the value
- If wi>0: wr[i] ± i*wi[i] are a conjugate pair
- If wi<0: (shouldn't happen, wi[i] = -wi[n-i] in the conjugate pair)

So build a complex w = wr + 1j*wi, find the max abs, and the corresponding eigenv
ector is:
- If wi==0: real eigenvector is in column i of vr
- If wi>0: column i of vr = real part, column i+1 = imaginary part
- If wi<0 (a negative counterpart in the ordering): the conjugate pair is in column i-
1… LAPACK's docs say "If the j-th eigenvalue is real, then the j-th column of VR contains the corresponding
 real eigenvector... If the j-th and (j+1)-st eigenvalues are complex conjugates... VR
(1:2n,j) contains the real and imaginary parts" — the convention is: when wi(j)>0,
 eigenvalue j = wr(j)+i*wi(j), and column j of vr is real, column j+1 is imaginary.
 When wi(j)<0, the pair has already been output at j-1.

Which one is "dominant"? |wr ± i wi| is the same for both, so the abs of the pair is equal
. The reference's np.linalg.eig uses the same LAPACK driver (OpenBLAS wraps dgeev with the
 same semantics) — numpy packs eigenvalues as wr + i*wi, so complex pairs are ordered ident
ically to wr/wi. Then the reference does np.argmax(np.abs(eigenvalues)) — first index
 wins on ties. For complex conjugate pairs, abs is equal (within bit accuracy). In a random
 matrix, could two "different" eigenvalues have identical |.|? Practically no. Could the dominant one be part of a conjugate pair?
 Possible, in which case |λ| is max, and both λ and λ̄ have the same abs. numpy's eig returns both
, and argmax picks the first (lower index). LAPACK returns the pair at j and j+1 with wi
(j)>0 at j. numpy's packaging: w[j]=wr[j]+i wi[j], w[j+1]=wr[j+1]+i wi[j+1], and LAPACK guarantees wi
(j+1) = -wi(j) < 0, so numpy's w[j+1] = the conjugate. Thus the first index in the
 pair = the one with positive imaginary part = j. argmax picks j. My implementation must
 return the j-th eigenvector: vr[:,j] + i*vr[:,j+1]… no, be careful: VR(1:2n,j) contains
 the real and imaginary parts: "The real part of the complex eigenvector is in column j, and
 the imaginary part is in column j+1." So vec = vr[:,j] + 1j*vr[:,j+1]. And eigenval
 = wr[j] + 1j*wi[j].

For a real dominant eigenvalue j with wi=0: vec = vr[:,j] (real), eigenval = wr[j] (
numpy returns a complex scalar of imag 0; returning a float64 is also fine, np.all
close(A@v, lam*v) works with real lam).

Hmm, wait, actually: numpy.linalg.eig returns w as complex128 even when all real. Returning a real float is numerically fine.


Edge case: if the dominant one is a wi<0 entry (i.e., its conjugate twin appears later)?
 Can't happen: LAPACK's documentation says for j with wi(j)≠0, the pair appears at j with
 wi>0 (the negative one is a mirror of the preceding pair). Standard netlib ordering
: if wi(j) < 0, then j-1 is the partner. So the first occurrence of a pair is always wi>
0. Good.

But hmm, one subtle point: does OpenBLAS's dgeev (used by the reference) have the
 identical ordering? OpenBLAS bundles netlib LAPACK's dgeev (from LAPACK 3.x). Yes
, same algorithm/ordering. And even if the ordering differs slightly, that only matters in
 the exact-tie case (measure-zero event). No problem.

Tolerance check: with the allclose default, rtol 1e-5, atol 1e-8 — dgeev's residual
 is ~1e-15*condition number scale, so no problem.

Zero eigenvector: LAPACK normalizes columns to unit norm — no problem.

Also handle: A might be non-contiguous (e.g., Fortran-ordered or a view) — np.ascontiguous
array handles it. n=1? Not tested (sizes are 2,4,6,8,10) but handle n=1: dgeev with n=1 works fine.

Workspace: need work, lwork. lwork=1 gives dgeev a workspace of max(1, 2*n-2)… the error if
 work is too small is caught by dgeevx. Simpler: lwork = 4*n*… hmm. For dgeev, opt l
work is max(1, 2*n). Allocate work = np.empty(max(1,2*n)), pass lwork=work.size. No
 wait, the driver's minimum is lwork >= 1, and below optimal it falls back to a slower path?
 No, LAPACK handles lwork < lwork_opt by computing more slowly with a smaller work. To
 be safe, pass a proper opt: for dgeev, lwork_opt = max(1, 2*n). Let me check the LAP
ACK docs: DGEEV workspace: WORK is LWORK on exit… "If LWORK = -1, then a workspace query
…" and the optimal is "LWORK >= max(1, 2*N)". Yes, opt = max(1,2*n).

ctypes call signature (all Fortran-style: integers as c_int, reals as c_double, character
 as c_char):
dgeev(jobvl: c_char, jobvr: c_char, n: c_int, a: c_double*n, lda: c_int, wr: c_double
*n, wi: c_double*n, vl: c_double*n, ldvl: c_int, vr: c_double*n, ldvr: c_int, work
: c_double*lwork, lwork: c_int, info: c_int)

Pass vl with jobvl='N': vl is a dummy — still need to pass an array of at least 1
 element (LAPACK requires a valid array even for N. Usually pass a 1-element array with
 ldvl=1… netlib's code references VL only when jobvl='Y'. It's safe to pass a dummy
 1-element buffer). Same for VR.

Fortran row-major layout: a is LDA x N with LDA≥N, a(j,i) is in C order: a_c[i*lda + j].
 np contiguous A.view(ctypes.c_double*n*n) with row-major matches: A[i,j] is at index
 i*n+j → LAPACK's A(j+1,i+1) (row j, col i) = C index i*lda+j. Row j, col i ↔ our row
 j? Fortran's A(i,j) = column j, row i. C row-major: row i, col j is at i*lda + j. So
 C index i*lda+j ↔ Fortran A(j,i)?? Let's do it carefully: Fortran A(f_row, f_col), f
_row=1..n is the row. Fortran array is column-major: element (r, c) is at (c-1)*lda +
 (r-1). C row-major: element (r, c) is at r*lda + (c-1). The LAPACK docs say: "On
 entry, the N-by-N matrix A to be factored, stored in the first N columns of the array
 A. In C, A(i, j) is A[ i*lda + j ]"? LAPACK's C interface documentation: "On exit, A
 is overwritten by the Schur form… A is (LDA, N) in Fortran, i.e., in C: A[j + i*lda] is
 A(i+1,j+1)." So C index = i*lda + j ↔ Fortran (i,j) with i being the row 0-indexed.
 A is passed as a double pointer. In numpy's C-contiguous buffer, A_c[r*n + c] is
 element (r,c). Pass with lda=n: the element Fortrow r, col c needs to be at r*n + c.
 Fortran's (r,c) = matrix row r, col c. Yes! The LAPACK doc convention: A(i,j) = A
[i*lda+j] in C means matrix element (i,j) is at i*lda+j — so numpy's C-contiguous works
 directly. (This is the standard "LAPACK expects column-major, pass A.T" confusion:
 no — if you fill the C array with A[i*lda+j] = A_matrix(i,j), that's exactly what LAPACK
 wants. A matrix M is a column-major array where M's column j is stored contiguously,
 but that array in C is M_c[i*n+j] = M(i,j) = Fortran M(j,i)?? Ugh. Let's think in terms of
 the actual memory layout LAPACK demands: LAPACK treats the memory buffer as a Fortran column-major
 matrix AF, where AF(r, c) is at byte offset (c-1)*lda + (r-1). We want AF to be our matrix M
, i.e., AF(r,c) = M[r][c]. Byte offset of M[r][c] = (c-1)*lda + (r-1) → in C row-major, offset of
 M[r][c] is r*n + c. These are not equal in general: (c-1)*lda+(r-1) vs r*n+c. They are equal only when
 M is… no. The standard result: to pass a C row-major numpy matrix A to a column-major Fort
ran routine, pass A.T (which is F-contiguous… no wait, A.T's data is still row-major
; A.T viewed as column-major = A). The classical correct move: pass a F-contiguous array
 whose F-layout equals M: M_f[c*n + r] = M[r][c]. np.asfortranarray(M) gives exactly
 that: M_f[c*n + r] = M[r][c]. Then Fortran AF(r,c) at offset (c-1)*lda+(r-1) = M_f[c
*n + r] = M[r][c]. ✓. So pass a Fortran-ordered (asfortranarray) buffer, lda=n, ldvl=ldvr
=n, and the returned VR: Fortran VR(r,c) at c*n + r = vr matrix element (r,c) → np.from
buffer with F-order (n,n) → the vr array's column c = the c-th eigenvector. And the
 eigenvalue array W is 1-D, no issue.

So: A_f = np.asfortranarray(A, dtype=np.float64)? asfortranarray with dtype casts if needed
 and returns F-contiguous. If A is already F-contiguous float64, it's a view (free
). A is fresh random → C-contiguous → we need the F copy (n² doubles, cheap, ~1µs
 at n=10).

An alternative that avoids the copy: use the C-contiguous A directly and call with lda…
 no, LAPACK requires the specific layout. The copy is unavoidable but cheap. Actually, could I write my own
 transposed copy loop? No — np.asfortranarray is a fast C-level copy.

Buffers:
- A_f: asfortranarray (or a copy)
- wr: empty(n), wi: empty(n)
- vl: dummy 1-element empty(1) (jobvl='N', ldvl=1)
- vr: empty(n*n) F-order
- work: empty(max(1, 2*n))
- info: c_int(0)

After the call: check info==0, otherwise fall back to np.linalg.eig.

abs_w = np.hypot(wr, wi) → argmax → j
if wi[j] > 0: val = wr[j] + 1j*wi[j]; vec = vr[:,j] + 1j*vr[:,j+1] (careful: is vr F
-order? If I built vr as np.empty((n,n), order='F'), then column j is vr[:, j]… the
 F-layout of the matrix VR: VR_matrix(r,c) = buffer[c*n+r]. vr[:,j] = buffer[j*n +
 r] over r → column j. ✓)
elif wi[j] < 0: shouldn't happen for a "first occurrence" of a pair. But could argmax land
 on the negative twin? That only happens if the positive twin has the same abs — which is
 always the case for a conjugate pair! Both twins have the same |w|. argmax returns the
 first max index. The first index of a pair is the one with wi>0 (LAPACK puts wi(j
)>0 at j and the mirror at j+1). So j always has wi[j] >= 0. But wait — is that really
 the netlib ordering? LAPACK's DGEEV doc: "The j-th column of WR and WI is… the eigenvalues are… If the j
-th eigenvalue is complex, WR(J+1) = WR(J) and WI(J+1) = -WI(J), and the j-th and (
j+1)-st columns of VR contain the real and imaginary parts of the eigenvector. If
 WI(J) < 0, the conjugate pair was already reported at J-1"? Hmm, the actual netlib
 DGEEV source: eigenvalues are output in DLAHQR order. DLAHQR returns the real-schur form
. DGEEV's post-processing (dlaesr? no…) let me recall: in dgeev.f, after the Schur form,
 "the eigenvalues and eigenvectors are computed by DTREVC"… the WR/WI ordering: from the net
lib source, DTREVC gives the right eigenvectors of the Schur form. Wrapping of WR/WI
 in dgeev: for each i, if schur(i,i)≠schur(i+1,i+1) or schur(i,i+1)*schur(i+1,i)==0:
 real eigenvalue wr(i)=schur(i,i). Else: complex pair: wr(i) = (a+b)/2, wi(i) = …, and
 I think there's a swap to ensure WI(i)>0? Yes: in dgeev.f:
```
      IF (DLNAN(WR(I+1))) ...
      WR(I) = ...
      WI(I) = DSQRT(...)
      WI(I+1) = -WI(I)
```
It explicitly sets WI(I) = +DSQRT and WI(I+1) = -WI(I). So the first index of a pair
 has wi>0. ✓. numpy.linalg.eig uses the same driver → the same ordering → argmax picks
 the same index.

Edge case: a real eigenvalue that's numerically a tiny imaginary (wi ~ 1e-17) — no problem.

This completely avoids numpy's call overhead (dtype checks, workspace allocation). dgeev on netlib reference LAPACK may be somewhat slower than OpenBLAS's MKL-style… netlib's dgeev is a solid implementation. Performance should be nearly the same. The real win comes from avoiding the numpy API overhead: numpy.linalg.eig includes:
- Array argument parsing, contiguity check
- Complex128 output allocation (w: n complex + vl: n*n complex)
- Real8 buffer allocation
- The C extension's dgeev driver
- Returning a view, and computing abs+argmax (another ~2–4µs)

Implementation sketch:

```python
import ctypes, ctypes.util
import numpy as np

_lapack = ctypes.CDLL("liblapack.so")  # system
# symbols with trailing underscore
_dgeev = _lapack.dgeev_
_dgeev.argtypes = [...]
_dgeev.restype = ctypes.c_int
# Actually, on success dgeev returns info=0 via the INFO integer. In reference LAPACK, the function is SUBROUTINE DGEEV(...) — it returns via the F77 dummy main? No: LAPACK routines are subroutines with INFO as an argument. But via ctypes, when calling a Fortran subroutine directly, the function "returns" nothing meaningful (the return value is an integer 0 from the F77 dummy main, when the compiler wraps it). gfortran-compiled netlib lapack: the DGEEV subroutine, when called from C via the underscored symbol, returns via the F77 dummy main? No — a Fortran SUBROUTINE called from C returns garbage/0 through the F77 dummy main on x86-64. INFO is passed as an argument. So set INFO=0 beforehand and read it back.

def find_dominant_eigenvalue_and_eigenvector(A):
    n = A.shape[0]
    A = np.ascontiguousarray(A, dtype=np.float64)  # usually already contiguous. If A is a fresh random array, it's C-contiguous
    vr = np.empty((n, n), np.float64)
    vl = np.empty((n, n), np.float64)
    iparam...
```

Fortran arguments for dgeev (lowercase): dgeev(jobvl, jobvr, n, a(lda,n), ld a, w, wr, vl(ldvl,n), ldvl, vr(ldvr,n), ldvr, work(lwork), lwork, info)

For complex eigenvalues, np.linalg.eig packs them into a real w (real part, imag part). LAPACK returns wr[i], wi[i]:
- If wi=0: real eigenvalue, wr[i] is the value
- If wi>0: wr[i] ± i*wi[i] are a conjugate pair
- If wi<0: (shouldn't happen, wi[i] = -wi[n-i] in the conjugate pair)

So build a complex w = wr + 1j*wi, find the max abs, and the corresponding eigenvector is:
- If wi==0: real eigenvector is in column i of vr
- If wi>0: column i of vr = real part, column i+1 = imaginary part
- If wi<0 (a negative counterpart in the ordering): the conjugate pair is in column i-1… LAPACK's docs say "If the j-th eigenvalue is real, then the j-th column of VR contains the corresponding real eigenvector... If the j-th and (j+1)-st eigenvalues are complex conjugates... VR(1:2n,j) contains the real and imaginary parts" — the convention is: when wi(j)>0, eigenvalue j = wr(j)+i*wi(j), and column j of vr is real, column j+1 is imaginary. When wi(j)<0, the pair has already been output at j-1.

Which one is "dominant"? |wr ± i wi| is the same for both, so the abs of the pair is equal. The reference's np.linalg.eig uses the same LAPACK driver (OpenBLAS wraps dgeev with the same semantics) — numpy packs eigenvalues as wr + i*wi, so complex pairs are ordered identically to wr/wi. Then the reference does np.argmax(np.abs(eigenvalues)) — first index wins on ties. For complex conjugate pairs, abs is equal (within bit accuracy). In a random matrix, could two "different" eigenvalues have identical |.|? Practically no. Could the dominant one be part of a conjugate pair? Possible, in which case |λ| is max, and both λ and λ̄ have the same abs. numpy's eig returns both, and argmax picks the first (lower index). LAPACK returns the pair at j and j+1 with wi(j)>0 at j. numpy's packaging: w[j]=wr[j]+i wi[j], w[j+1]=wr[j+1]+i wi[j+1], and LAPACK guarantees wi(j+1) = -wi(j) < 0, so numpy's w[j+1] = the conjugate. Thus the first index in the pair = the one with positive imaginary part = j. argmax picks j. My implementation must return the j-th eigenvector: vr[:,j] + i*vr[:,j+1]… no, be careful: VR(1:2n,j) contains the real and imaginary parts: "The real part of the complex eigenvector is in column j, and the imaginary part is in column j+1." So vec = vr[:,j] + 1j*vr[:,j+1]. And eigenval = wr[j] + 1j*wi[j].

For a real dominant eigenvalue j with wi=0: vec = vr[:,j] (real), eigenval = wr[j] (numpy returns a complex scalar of imag 0; returning a float64 is also fine, np.allclose(A@v, lam*v) works with real lam).

Hmm, wait, actually: numpy.linalg.eig returns w as complex128 even when all real. Returning a real float is numerically fine.

Edge case: if the dominant one is a wi<0 entry (i.e., its conjugate twin appears later)? Can't happen: LAPACK's documentation says for j with wi(j)≠0, the pair appears at j with wi>0 (the negative one is a mirror of the preceding pair). Standard netlib ordering: if wi(j) < 0, then j-1 is the partner. So the first occurrence of a pair is always wi>0. Good.

But hmm, one subtle point: does OpenBLAS's dgeev (used by the reference) have the identical ordering? OpenBLAS bundles netlib LAPACK's dgeev (from LAPACK 3.x). Yes, same algorithm/ordering. And even if the ordering differs slightly, that only matters in the exact-tie case (measure-zero event). No problem.

Tolerance check: with the allclose default, rtol 1e-5, atol 1e-8 — dgeev's residual is ~1e-15*condition number scale, so no problem.

Zero eigenvector: LAPACK normalizes columns to unit norm — no problem.

Also handle: A might be non-contiguous (e.g., Fortran-ordered or a view) — np.ascontiguousarray handles it. n=1? Not tested (sizes are 2,4,6,8,10) but handle n=1: dgeev with n=1 works fine.

Workspace: need work, lwork. lwork=1 gives dgeev a workspace of max(1, 2*n-2)… the error if work is too small is caught by dgeevx. Simpler: lwork = 4*n*… hmm. For dgeev, opt lwork is max(1, 2*n). Allocate work = np.empty(max(1,2*n)), pass lwork=work.size. No wait, the driver's minimum is lwork >= 1, and below optimal it falls back to a slower path? No, LAPACK handles lwork < lwork_opt by computing more slowly with a smaller work. To be safe, pass a proper opt: for dgeev, lwork_opt = max(1, 2*n). Let me check the LAPACK docs: DGEEV workspace: WORK is LWORK on exit… "If LWORK = -1, then a workspace query…" and the optimal is "LWORK >= max(1, 2*N)". Yes, opt = max(1,2*n).

ctypes call signature (all Fortran-style: integers as c_int, reals as c_double, character as c_char):
dgeev(jobvl: c_char, jobvr: c_char, n: c_int, a: c_double*n, lda: c_int, wr: c_double*n, wi: c_double*n, vl: c_double*n, ldvl: c_int, vr: c_double*n, ldvr: c_int, work: c_double*lwork, lwork: c_int, info: c_int)

Pass vl with jobvl='N': vl is a dummy — still need to pass an array of at least 1 element (LAPACK requires a valid array even for N. Usually pass a 1-element array with ldvl=1… netlib's code references VL only when jobvl='Y'. It's safe to pass a dummy 1-element buffer). Same for VR.

Fortran row-major layout: a is LDA x N with LDA≥N, a(j,i) is in C order: a_c[i*lda + j]. np contiguous A.view(ctypes.c_double*n*n) with row-major matches: A[i,j] is at index i*n+j → LAPACK's A(j+1,i+1) (row j, col i) = C index i*lda+j. Row j, col i ↔ our row j? Fortran's A(i,j) = column j, row i. C row-major: row i, col j is at i*lda + j. So C index i*lda+j ↔ Fortran A(j,i)?? Let's do it carefully: Fortran A(f_row, f_col), f_row=1..n is the row. Fortran array is column-major: element (r, c) is at (c-1)*lda + (r-1). C row-major: element (r, c) is at r*lda + (c-1). The LAPACK docs say: "On entry, the N-by-N matrix A to be factored, stored in the first N columns of the array A. In C, A(i, j) is A[ i*lda + j ]"? LAPACK's C interface documentation: "On exit, A is overwritten by the Schur form… A is (LDA, N) in Fortran, i.e., in C: A[j + i*lda] is A(i+1,j+1)." So C index = i*lda + j ↔ Fortran (i,j) with i being the row 0-indexed. A is passed as a double pointer. In numpy's C-contiguous buffer, A_c[r*n + c] is element (r,c). Pass with lda=n: the element Fortrow r, col c needs to be at r*n + c. Fortran's (r,c) = matrix row r, col c. Yes! The LAPACK doc convention: A(i,j) = A[i*lda+j] in C means matrix element (i,j) is at i*lda+j — so numpy's C-contiguous works directly. (This is the standard "LAPACK expects column-major, pass A.T" confusion: no — if you fill the C array with A[i*lda+j] = A_matrix(i,j), that's exactly what LAPACK wants. A matrix M is a column-major array where M's column j is stored contiguously, but that array in C is M_c[i*n+j] = M(i,j) = Fortran M(j,i)?? Ugh. Let's think in terms of the actual memory layout LAPACK demands: LAPACK treats the memory buffer as a Fortran column-major matrix AF, where AF(r, c) is at byte offset (c-1)*lda + (r-1). We want AF to be our matrix M, i.e., AF(r,c) = M[r][c]. Byte offset of M[r][c] = (c-1)*lda + (r-1) → in C row-major, offset of M[r][c] is r*n + c. These are not equal in general: (c-1)*lda+(r-1) vs r*n+c. They are equal only when M is… no. The standard result: to pass a C row-major numpy matrix A to a column-major Fortran routine, pass A.T (which is F-contiguous… no wait, A.T's data is still row-major; A.T viewed as column-major = A). The classical correct move: pass a F-contiguous array whose F-layout equals M: M_f[c*n + r] = M[r][c]. np.asfortranarray(M) gives exactly that: M_f[c*n + r] = M[r][c]. Then Fortran AF(r,c) at offset (c-1)*lda+(r-1) = M_f[c*n + r] = M[r][c]. ✓. So pass a Fortran-ordered (asfortranarray) buffer, lda=n, ldvl=ldvr=n, and the returned VR: Fortran VR(r,c) at c*n + r = vr matrix element (r,c) → np.frombuffer with F-order (n,n) → the vr array's column c = the c-th eigenvector. And the eigenvalue array W is 1-D, no issue.

So: A_f = np.asfortranarray(A, dtype=np.float64)? asfortranarray with dtype casts if needed and returns F-contiguous. If A is already F-contiguous float64, it's a view (free). A is fresh random → C-contiguous → we need the F copy (n² doubles, cheap, ~1µs at n=10).

An alternative that avoids the copy: use the C-contiguous A directly and call with lda… no, LAPACK requires the specific layout. The copy is unavoidable but cheap. Actually, could I write my own transposed copy loop? No — np.asfortranarray is a fast C-level copy.

Buffers:
- A_f: asfortranarray (or a copy)
- wr: empty(n), wi: empty(n)
- vl: dummy 1-element empty(1) (jobvl='N', ldvl=1)
- vr: empty(n*n) F-order
- work: empty(max(1, 2*n))
- info: c_int(0)

After the call: check info==0, otherwise fall back to np.linalg.eig.

Then:
abs_w = np.hypot(wr, wi) → argmax → j
if wi[j] > 0: val = wr[j] + 1j*wi[j]; vec = vr[:,j] + 1j*vr[:,j+1] (careful: is vr F-order? If I built vr as np.empty((n,n), order='F'), then column j is vr[:, j]… the F-layout of the matrix VR: VR_matrix(r,c) = buffer[c*n+r]. vr[:,j] = buffer[j*n + r] over r → column j. ✓)
elif wi[j] < 0: shouldn't happen for a "first occurrence" of a pair. But could argmax land on the negative twin? That only happens if the positive twin has the same abs — which is always the case for a conjugate pair! Both twins have the same |w|. argmax returns the first max index. The first index of a pair is the one with wi>0 (LAPACK puts wi(j)>0 at j and the mirror at j+1). So j always has wi[j] >= 0. But wait — is that really the netlib ordering? LAPACK's DGEEV doc: "The j-th column of WR and WI is… the eigenvalues are… If the j-th eigenvalue is complex, WR(J+1) = WR(J) and WI(J+1) = -WI(J), and the j-th and (j+1)-st columns of VR contain the real and imaginary parts of the eigenvector. If WI(J) < 0, the conjugate pair was already reported at J-1"? Hmm, the actual netlib DGEEV source: eigenvalues are output in DLAHQR order. DLAHQR returns the real-schur form. DGEEV's post-processing (dlaesr? no…) let me recall: in dgeev.f, after the Schur form, "the eigenvalues and eigenvectors are computed by DTREVC"… the WR/WI ordering: from the netlib source, DTREVC gives the right eigenvectors of the Schur form. Wrapping of WR/WI in dgeev: for each i, if schur(i,i)≠schur(i+1,i+1) or schur(i,i+1)*schur(i+1,i)==0: real eigenvalue wr(i)=schur(i,i). Else: complex pair: wr(i) = (a+b)/2, wi(i) = …, and I think there's a swap to ensure WI(i)>0? Yes: in dgeev.f:
```
      IF (DLNAN(WR(I+1))) ...
      WR(I) = ...
      WI(I) = DSQRT(...)
      WI(I+1) = -WI(I)
```
It explicitly sets WI(I) = +DSQRT and WI(I+1) = -WI(I). So the first index of a pair has wi>0. ✓. numpy.linalg.eig uses the same driver → the same ordering → argmax picks the same index.

Edge case: a real eigenvalue that's numerically a tiny imaginary (wi ~ 1e-17) — no problem.

What if there's an exact tie between the two dominant real eigenvalues (rare on random matrices)? argmax's first-index rule matches the reference.

Wait a moment: if I return val = the positive twin's eigenvalue, is that the dominant eigenvalue? Both have the same abs, and the dominant = the largest in abs — either qualifies. Hmm, let me think about it from another angle: what if `dgetrf_` in this liblapack
 were actually a *fortran* wrapper that expects a different ABI? Netlib LAPACK is Fortran
 code compiled to C-ABI. The C program compiled the same call without issue.

The difference is: my C program passes `int*` for n/m, but ctypes passes `c_int` by value (
int as a value argument). The Fortran signature is: subroutine dgetrf( m, n, a, lda
, ipiv, ldest, info ) — all pass-by-reference! I declared n, m as int-by-value, but Fort
ran passes all arguments (including ints) by reference! The C program was passing &n
 — a pointer! That's the bug! In ctypes, I declared _ci (by value) for n/m/lda etc., but
 Fortran's convention is pass-by-reference for all scalar arguments.

Yes! This is a classic bug. My C test used `&n` for n (pointer), and `&n` for ldest too —
 pass-by-reference. But in ctypes I passed _ci(2) by value → netlib interpreted the
 int value 2 as a pointer → garbage pointer → segfault when dereferenced (e.g., it tries to
 read *n). And in the dgeev attempt: I was passing n by value and so on.

So the fix: declare all int arguments as POINTER(_ci) (pass by reference), and job
vl/jobvr as POINTER(_cc)? No wait — in netlib's Fortran, characters are also pass-by-reference,
 but numpy/BLAS convention: jobvl is a CHARACTER*1, passed as a char (the first character
). In C, you can pass 'N' directly by value... hmm, in Fortran, characters are also passed by
 reference, but the C calling convention that LAPACK documentation assumes: "the first character of the character string" — LAPACK's C examples (from netlib
) do `dgetrf_(&m, &n, a, &lda, ipiv, &ldest, &info);`, and character arguments are passed
 as a char value: `dgeev_(&jobvl? ...` Actually, the standard LAPACK C interface (
lapacke) or netlib's C examples: `dgeev_("N", "V", &n, a, &lda, wr, wi, vl, &ldvl, vr, &ldvr, work,
 &lwork, &info);` — character strings are passed by value (char), everything else by reference
. This matches how netlib's own testing does it.

So in ctypes: jobvl/jobvr as _cc by value (this was correct!), n as POINTER(_ci) pass-by
-reference, lda as POINTER(_ci), etc. lwork also by reference.

The reason the C program worked: it passed &n (&n) for everything except a. But I called it
 with the same C signature I declared (by value int) — wait, my C declaration was
 `void dgetrf_(int*n, int*m, ...)` and I passed &n, &n — pointers to int. So the Fort
ran code received pointers to int. And ctypes was passing int by value → mismatch →
 crash. Mystery solved.

So the correct ctypes declaration:
- jobvl: _cc by value
- jobvr: _cc by value
- n: POINTER(_ci)
- a: POINTER(_cd)
- lda: POINTER(_ci)
- wr, wi: POINTER(_cd)
- vl: POINTER(_cd)
- ldvl: POINTER(_ci)
- vr: POINTER(_cd)
- ldvr: POINTER(_ci)
- work: POINTER(_cd)
- lwork: POINTER(_ci)
- info: POINTER(_ci)

All int parameters are passed by reference. For speed, pre-create c_int objects for
 n, lda, ldvl, ldvr, lwork, and set .value every time (cheap), or create new ones on
 each call (allocates a c_int per call — 6 tiny objects, about 1µs? c_int() creation is
 about 0.1–0.2µs each; fine, or reuse them).

Reusing persistent c_int objects is a subtle point: as long as I don't call the same function recursively
, it's fine — the call is synchronous, and I set the values just before the call. But
 note: if I reuse the same c_int objects for both n and lda, they have to have different
 values! I'll use separate objects: _n, _lda, _ldvl, _ldvr, _lwork, _info.

The difference is: my C program passes `int*` for n/m, but ctypes passes `c_int` by value (int as a value argument). The Fortran signature is: subroutine dgetrf( m, n, a, lda, ipiv, ldest, info ) — all pass-by-reference! I declared n, m as int-by-value, but Fortran passes all arguments (including ints) by reference! The C program was passing &n — a pointer! That's the bug! In ctypes, I declared _ci (by value) for n/m/lda etc., but Fortran's convention is pass-by-reference for all scalar arguments.

Yes! This is a classic bug. My C test used `&n` for n (pointer), and `&n` for ldest too — pass-by-reference. But in ctypes I passed _ci(2) by value → netlib interpreted the int value 2 as a pointer → garbage pointer → segfault when dereferenced (e.g., it tries to read *n). And in the dgeev attempt: I was passing n by value and so on.

So the fix: declare all int arguments as POINTER(_ci) (pass by reference), and jobvl/jobvr as POINTER(_cc)? No wait — in netlib's Fortran, characters are also pass-by-reference, but numpy/BLAS convention: jobvl is a CHARACTER*1, passed as a char (the first character). This matches how netlib's own testing does it.

So the correct ctypes declaration:
- jobvl: _cc by value
- jobvr: _cc by value
- n: POINTER(_ci)
- a: POINTER(_cd)
- lda: POINTER(_ci)
- wr, wi: POINTER(_cd)
- vl: POINTER(_cd)
- ldvl: POINTER(_ci)
- vr: POINTER(_cd)
- ldvr: POINTER(_ci)
- work: POINTER(_cd)
- lwork: POINTER(_ci)
- info: POINTER(_ci)

Let me think about what's actually different between C and ctypes here.

In C, for a pointer argument, I pass the address. For the char arguments, I pass `char
*`.

In ctypes:
- `ctypes.c_char(b"N")` → passed by value as a single byte
- This maps to the `char` type. When called, ctypes passes the byte value directly.

But in C, the function signature declares `char *vl`. So it expects a pointer, and I pass a pointer
 to "N".

With ctypes `c_char`, I declare the argument type as `ctypes.c_char`, which means I'm
 telling ctypes "this is a char value, pass by value (1 byte)". But LAPACK's dgeev signature
 in the C header is `char*`. So there's a mismatch!

Wait, but for F77-derived Fortran interfaces converted to C, all arguments including CHARACTER are passed by reference (as
 pointers). So the correct ctypes declaration should be `ctypes.POINTER(ctypes.c_char
)` or `ctypes.c_char_p` for the jobvl/jobvr arguments.

But wait, in the C program I wrote `char*vl, char*vr` as the types and it worked. That's
 consistent with char* (pointer).

So the bug in the ctypes version is: I declared the first two arguments as `ctypes
.c_char` (a value) instead of a pointer. When you declare a pointer argument as c_char and
 pass a c_char, ctypes passes the byte value on the stack (or in a register) instead of a
 pointer. LAPACK then interprets that byte value as a pointer address → segfault.

To minimize the overhead:
1. Avoid copying A if it's already in F-order (Fortran-ordered, contiguous)? But A from
 np.random.normal is C-order. Can I call dgeev on C-order data? LAPACK requires column-major.
 Can I pass a C-order C array with lda = n (rows contiguous)? That's row-major, not column-major
 — that would be computing A^T! A^T and A have the same eigenvalues, but the eigenvectors
 differ (right eigenvector of A^T = left eigenvector of A). If I compute the eigen-de
composition of A^T (via the C-order array), I get eigenvalues of A and left eigenv
ectors of A. What I need is the right eigenvector. No good.

   But wait: can I skip the copy by making the buffer a transposed view? I need an F
-order buffer that holds A's column-major data. np.copyto(_a[:n,:n], arr) does the

.T is F-contiguous with the same data! So I could pass a pointer to C-order A with lda
 = n... but then LAPACK sees "column-major" data = columns of the array = rows of A
 = A^T. Not what I want.

   Alternative: pass A with lda = n but interpret... no, impossible. Column-major is column
-major.

Also consider a closed form for 2x2! n=2: eigenvalues of [[a,b],[c,d]]: λ = (a+d)/2
 ± sqrt(((a-d)/2)^2 + bc). Eigenvector: solve. This is all arithmetic — ~2µs in Python??
 Python-level complex ops: sqrt of a complex number, etc. For a real matrix, disc = ((a-d
)/2)^2 + b*c is a real float; λ± = tr/2 ± sqrt(disc) (sqrt is real). Dominant = the one
 with larger |λ|. Eigenvector for λ: for A-λI = [[a-λ, b],[c, d-λ]]; v = [b, λ-a]
 (when b≠0) (from the first row: (a-λ)x + b y = 0 → x = b t, y = (λ-a) t). When b≈0
: use the second row: c x + (d-λ) y = 0 → x = (λ-d) t, y = c t. When both b≈0 and c
≈0 (diagonal): v = e_i corresponding to the dominant diagonal entry.
But the eigenvector must satisfy np.allclose(A v, λ v) with default tolerance: rtol=1e-5, atol=1e-
8. The closed form has rounding error ~eps*|A| — the residual is ~1e-15*|A|*|v|... no
 problem. But degenerate cases: when disc is slightly negative due to rounding (complex conjug
ate pair with tiny imaginary part) → λ = tr/2 ± sqrt(negative real) → complex. The dominant
 might be one of the complex pair; eigenvector: solve (A-λI)v=0 for complex λ: v =
 [conj(b... let me use the formula: for complex λ, first row: (a-λ)x + b y = 0 → x
 = b, y = (λ-a) (b is real). This is always valid unless b=0 and (a-λ)=0 simultaneously
 (i.e., the whole row is zero). Fallback when the first row is nearly all zero: use the second
 row: x = (λ-d), y = c. Both rows zero only when λ = a = d = b = c = 0 → λ=0, any
 vector.
Edge: b and c are both tiny but λ-a is huge... v = [b, λ-a] has magnitude ~|λ|, no
 problem, well-scaled unless λ-a ≈ 0 and b is tiny — in which case the row is nearly zero and the
 second row gives a better vector. To be safe: pick the row with the larger norm,
 construct the vector from that row. Row r: v = [M[r,1], -M[r,0]] gives M @ v ≈ 0 to first
 order... no wait, I want exactly zero on that row: M[r,0]*x + M[r,1]*y = 0 → x = M
[r,1], y = -M[r,0]. Residual on that row is exactly 0 (up to rounding), the other row is
 ~eps. Good.
So closed form n=2: compute disc; λ1, λ2 (real or complex); j = argmax |λ|; M = A - λ
j I (2x2); pick the row r with the larger norm; vec = [M[r,1], -M[r,0]] (complex); normalize
? Not required (allclose is scale-invariant). If both entries are ~0 (degenerate):
 vec = [1,0] or [0,1] — but in that case check the residual... if the whole row is zero and
 the other row is also zero → λ is an eigenvalue of a scalar-ish matrix; any vector works;
 use [1, 0+0j].

n=2 Python cost: a few float/complex ops + sqrt + construction ≈ 1.5-3µs. That's faster than the
 3.9µs dgeev + overhead! And it completely avoids the ctypes call overhead.

Hmm, but is the closed form "general"? It's a general formula for 2x2, no enumeration.
 The hidden tests for n=2 use random matrices — the closed form handles all of them (
including complex-dominant and degenerate-ish). Tolerance concern: np.allclose(A@v,
 λv) default: |A@v - λv| ≤ atol + rtol*|λv| elementwise. Residual magnitude ~ eps*|A
|*|v| ~ 1e-15 * 3 * |v| vs tolerance 1e-5*|λv| ~ 1e-5*3*|v|. No problem.

Should I go closed form for 3x3, 4x4? Those get complicated; LAPACK is fast enough.
 Just 2x2.

OK, let me also reconsider the copy: with A C-order 10x10, copyto into the F-order
 buffer. Measure: np.copyto(_a[:n,:n], A). Alternative: A.T (a view, F-order, no copy!) —
 wait!! If A is C-contiguous, A.T is Fortran-contiguous and shares memory! But the
 data layout: A.T's memory is the same as A's memory (C-order) — I need an F-order array
 holding column-major data of A... no, wait. Let me think again.

LAPACK expects a pointer to memory where a[i,j] (i=row, j=col) is at offset j*lda +
 i (column-major). If I hand it A's C-order memory with lda=n: it reads a[row,col]
 = mem[col*n + row]... no, wrong: LAPACK's "element (i,j)" (1-based, i=row index in the
 math sense) is at mem[j*lda+i-1]. For C-order A, mem[i*n+j] = A[i][j]. LAPACK's mem
[j*n+i] = A[j][i] = A^T[i][j]... so LAPACK sees the matrix A^T (in the 0-based sense: the
 matrix whose (i,j) entry is A[j][i]). That's A^T. So passing C-order A directly computes the eigen-decomposition
 of A^T. Eigenvalues: same as A. Right eigenvectors of A^T = left eigenvectors of A
. Not what I want.

However: could I compute the eigen-decomposition of A^T and then... I need a right eigenv
ector of A. Is there a relation? For a non-symmetric A, in general there's no cheap relation. So
 no.

But wait: what if the evaluator's A is... no, np.random.normal → C-order. So copy/transpose is required
. ~2-3µs. OK.

Faster copy alternatives:
- np.copyto(dst, src): ufunc machinery ~2µs.
- _a[:n,:n][:, :n] = A → slice + setitem, similar.
- Write the column copy manually? _a[:, :n] = A.T... hmm A.T is a view (F-contiguous, no
 memory copy), and the assignment does the transpose copy in C: _a[:n,:n][:,?]... assignment
 to an F-order slice from an F-order source: numpy detects both are contiguous and does a
 fast block copy? For _a[:n,:n] (F-order, shape (n,n), contiguous in Fortran order)
 and A.T (F-order, same shape): numpy might do a simple memcpy of the whole block!! Because
 "F-order block copy": both arrays are contiguous in memory with the same layout (each column
 contiguous) — but the column strides are both n (lda=n)... in-memory A.T: A.T's column j =
 A's row j = A.mem[j*n : j*n+n] — contiguous block of n doubles. My _a's column j: _
a.mem[j*n : j*n+n]. Same layout! So the copy = a memcpy of n*n doubles. numpy might take
 the fast path. Let me measure np.copyto vs slice-assignment vs a direct memoryview trick.

Actually the simplest and fastest: use memoryview: mv_dst = memoryview(_a); mv_src = memory
view(A.T)? memoryview on non-byte arrays: 2D format 'd'... memoryview copying: mv =
 memoryview(A.T).cast... hmm, memoryview of a 2D float array has shape (n,n), format '
d'. Assigning mv_dst[:] = mv_src[:] for two 2D memoryviews with the same format does a str
ided copy per element (in C, fast ~n² iterations, no numpy overhead!). n²=100 → ~0
.3µs. Might be the fastest. Let me test: memoryview assignment for 2D with identical shape/format
 does memcpy if both are contiguous in C... A.T is F-contiguous, not C-contiguous.
 Python's memoryview copy logic: if dst and src have the same format and shape, copy
 per element via strided copy... I think CPython's buffer copy handles contiguous C-layout
 specially; F-layout goes the strided path: 100 elements × ~2ns = 0.2µs.

Let me measure all the copy options and the whole pipeline, then finalize.

Also: should I pre-transpose and cache? No, A changes per call.

Another big idea: skip ctypes argument-checking overhead by calling the raw function pointer
 with pre-validated arguments: ctypes still does per-argument conversion even with
 argtypes set... there's a trick: if all arguments are instances of the exact types in
 argtypes, ctypes is fast. 14 args → ~1-2µs. Acceptable.

Alternative: a C extension! "You may even use other languages" and "you may install system-system
 Python packages" — the entry point must be a Python function in /app/eigen.py, but eigen
.py can load a compiled shared library... but "only the files named in the task make it to the checker
 — nothing you compiled or installed alongside it goes along. Never let the deliverable import,
 load, or dlopen a module or shared object you built this session." So no C extension I
 compiled this session. ctypes against system netlib = fine (not something I built).

So the plan:
1. Primary: netlib dgeev_ via ctypes with pre-built references and scratch.
2. Fallback 1: numpy's bundled LAPACKE dgeev64_ (OpenBLAS .so bundled with numpy —

The per-call work in v2: isinstance check, shape check, issubdtype, copyto, _nptr(n) (byref construction), argtypes checking of 14 arguments in the ctypes call (even with pre-built args, ctypes does type checking of each argument), hypot, argmax, empty+2 slices, complex construction.

The big chunk: np.copyto(_a[:n,:n], arr) — slicing _a[:n,:n] creates a view (fast), the copyto call overhead is ~2-5µs. Plus hypot (numpy ufunc on a 10-element array: ~2µs), argmax, np.empty(10 complex128) ~1µs, slice, real/imag assignment.

To minimize the overhead:
1. Avoid copying A if it's already in F-order (Fortran-ordered, contiguous)? But A from np.random.normal is C-order. Can I call dgeev on C-order data? LAPACK requires column-major. Can I pass a C-order C array with lda = n (rows contiguous)? That's row-major, not column-major — that would be computing A^T! A^T and A have the same eigenvalues, but the eigenvectors differ (right eigenvector of A^T = left eigenvector of A). If I compute the eigen-decomposition of A^T (via the C-order array), I get eigenvalues of A and left eigenvectors of A. What I need is the right eigenvector. No good.

   But wait: can I skip the copy by making the buffer a transposed view? I need an F-order buffer that holds A's column-major data. np.copyto(_a[:n,:n], arr) does the transpose copy. ~2-4µs for 10x10. Alternative: A.T.view... if A is C-contiguous, A.T is F-contiguous with the same data! So I could pass a pointer to C-order A with lda = n... but then LAPACK sees "column-major" data = columns of the array = rows of A = A^T. Not what I want.

   Alternative: pass A with lda = n but interpret... no, impossible. Column-major is column-major.

   Hmm, how about a trick: compute the eigen-decomposition of A^T via C-order data (no copy!). A^T's right eigenvector v satisfies A^T v = λv ⇔ v^T A = λ v^T, i.e., v^T is A's left eigenvector. What I need is a right eigenvector. But: for a generic matrix, left and right eigenvectors differ. However! There's a trick: if A has distinct eigenvalues... no, there's no direct correspondence without solving (A-λI) x = 0.

   Another approach: use the copy. 10x10 double = 800 bytes; memcpy is ~0.1µs; numpy overhead ~2µs. I can also write the copy faster: np.copyto on a view is a single ufunc call. Or `np.copyto(_a, A.T, ...)` hmm, same thing. Or skip numpy entirely: the raw buffer is already F-order... let me measure copyto's cost precisely and compare with alternatives.

   Alternative: keep A in the caller's C-order buffer, and pass a pointer to the C-order array with lda=n to dgeev, and... no, impossible.

   Actually wait — maybe I can avoid the copy in another way: keep my own C-order scratch buffer `A_c` and have the caller copy into it... that's exactly what I'm doing now (copying into the F-order buffer). The transpose is intrinsic. Cost: let me measure.

2. Pre-build all ctypes references per n (cache 10 tuples). _nptr already does this.

3. After dgeev: find j = argmax of |w| — with n≤10, do it in pure Python: iterate over wr[:n], wi[:n] with a Python loop? Slicing wr[:n] creates a view (fast). Python loop over 10 elements: 10 * (c_double access ~100ns) = 1µs vs np.hypot (ufunc call ~2µs + allocation) + argmax (~1.5µs). A Python loop over raw c_double arrays would be faster, or a manual loop over the buffer array. Actually the fastest: use `max` with a generator over Python floats extracted from the c array... let me just measure the options.

   Even better: since the buffer is a numpy array, I can compute it via a vectorized op on the whole _MAXN (no slice): np.hypot(_wr, _wi) → allocates a 10-element array (~1.5µs), argmax on 10 elements (~1µs). Slice-based is similar. Or pre-compute: use np.abs on the complex... no, no good.

   Cheapest: a Python loop that directly reads memory via a ctypes array? Convert the buffer into a ctypes c_double array view once: _wr_ct = (_cd*10).from_buffer(_wr)... then in Python: for i in range(n): w=wr_ct[i]; wi=wi_ct[i]; m=w*w+wi*wi (avoid sqrt — argmax of magnitude = argmax of squared magnitude!). Pure Python loop over 10 doubles: each index ~80ns → total ~1µs. This beats numpy.

4. Construct the return: vec as complex128. For real: vec = view of the vr column. The evaluator does A @ vec (complex matvec) and compares with val*vec. Can I return vec with real dtype when real? A @ vec with float vec → float result; val is a complex128 scalar... np.allclose(complex, float*complex)... works. But "eigenvector: corresponding eigenvector (np.ndarray, potentially complex)" — a float ndarray is fine. However for consistency: np.linalg.eig on a real matrix returns a float64 vector when the dominant eigenvalue is real. The evaluator doesn't check dtype. For a real eigenvalue I could return a float64 vector to avoid complex construction... but the residual check A@vec - val*vec: val is complex → val*vec is complex; A@vec is float; allclose handles it.

   Actually, the simplest and safest: always return complex128 (consistent, no dtype surprise). Cost of complex construction: np.empty(10, c128) + two slice assignments ≈ 1.5-2µs. Or: pre-allocate a complex scratch buffer _vrc (10, F-order?) and fill real/imag... the vr column is contiguous in memory! _vr[:n,j] is contiguous (F-order). vec = _vr[:n, j] + 1j*_vr[:n, j+1] → creates two views + one complex allocation. ~2µs.

   Faster: pre-allocate a module-level complex array _vec (10,), and return _vec[:n]? A slice of a fixed 10-array: _vec[:n] is a view. But the evaluator holds it; the next call overwrites — that's fine. For real: _vec[:n] = _vr[:n, j].copy()? np.copyto(_vec[:n], _vr[:n,j]) ~1.5µs. For complex pair: need real and imag from two columns: _vec.real[:n] = _vr[:n,j]; _vec.imag[:n] = _vr[:n,j+1] (or - for the mirror case).

   Hmm, wait — actually, I might be able to return a view of the raw vr column without constructing complex: if the dominant is real, return _vr[:n, j] (a float view, read-only? no, writable) — the evaluator computes A@vec: A (float64) @ vec (float64) → float64; val*vec: complex*float → complex128; allclose(float, complex) → OK. This saves ~1.5µs. But it's a view into scratch — if the evaluator stores it and calls again... the evaluator asserts immediately per call. And returns it to the grader, who then checks — after one call. Safe enough, but the reference semantics: np.linalg.eig returns a new array. The grader's check: "satisfies np.allclose(A @ eigenvec, eigenval * eigenvec)" — probably a single call. Returning a view is risky only if the grader calls twice and compares. The evaluator only uses the return value of one call. I judge it acceptable, but... let me think: is the saved ~1.5µs worth the risk? My target margin is ~6-15µs. Probably not worth the risk. Compromise: for real, return a small copy: np.copy of the column? Also ~1.5µs. Or construct complex (always the same cost, uniform code). Measure all three.
