Also consider a closed form for 2x2! n=2: eigenvalues of [[a,b],[c,d]]: λ = (a+d)/2 ± sqrt(((a-d)/2)^2 + bc). Eigenvector: solve. This is all arithmetic — ~2µs in Python?? Python-level complex ops: sqrt of a complex number, etc. For a real matrix, disc = ((a-d)/2)^2 + b*c is a real float; λ± = tr/2 ± sqrt(disc) (sqrt is real). Dominant = the one with larger |λ|. Eigenvector for λ: for A-λI = [[a-λ, b],[c, d-λ]]; v = [b, λ-a] (when b≠0) (from the first row: (a-λ)x + b y = 0 → x = b t, y = (λ-a) t). When b≈0: use the second row: c x + (d-λ) y = 0 → x = (λ-d) t, y = c t. When both b≈0 and c≈0 (diagonal): v = e_i corresponding to the dominant diagonal entry.
But the eigenvector must satisfy np.allclose(A v, λ v) with default tolerance: rtol=1e-5, atol=1e-8. The closed form has rounding error ~eps*|A| — the residual is ~1e-15*|A|*|v|... no problem. But degenerate cases: when disc is slightly negative due to rounding (complex conjugate pair with tiny imaginary part) → λ = tr/2 ± sqrt(negative real) → complex. The dominant might be one of the complex pair; eigenvector: solve (A-λI)v=0 for complex λ: v = [conj(b... let me use the formula: for complex λ, first row: (a-λ)x + b y = 0 → x = b, y = (λ-a) (b is real). This is always valid unless b=0 and (a-λ)=0 simultaneously (i.e., the whole row is zero). Fallback when the first row is nearly all zero: use the second row: x = (λ-d), y = c. Both rows zero only when λ = a = d = b = c = 0 → λ=0, any vector.
Edge: b and c are both tiny but λ-a is huge... v = [b, λ-a] has magnitude ~|λ|, no problem, well-scaled unless λ-a ≈ 0 and b is tiny — in which case the row is nearly zero and the second row gives a better vector. To be safe: pick the row with the larger norm, construct the vector from that row. Row r: v = [M[r,1], -M[r,0]] gives M @ v ≈ 0 to first order... no wait, I want exactly zero on that row: M[r,0]*x + M[r,1]*y = 0 → x = M[r,1], y = -M[r,0]. Residual on that row is exactly 0 (up to rounding), the other row is ~eps. Good.
So closed form n=2: compute disc; λ1, λ2 (real or complex); j = argmax |λ|; M = A - λj I (2x2); pick the row r with the larger norm; vec = [M[r,1], -M[r,0]] (complex); normalize? Not required (allclose is scale-invariant). If both entries are ~0 (degenerate): vec = [1,0] or [0,1] — but in that case check the residual... if the whole row is zero and the other row is also zero → λ is an eigenvalue of a scalar-ish matrix; any vector works; use [1, 0+0j].

n=2 Python cost: a few float/complex ops + sqrt + construction ≈ 1.5-3µs. That's faster than the 3.9µs dgeev + overhead! And it completely avoids the ctypes call overhead.

Hmm, but is the closed form "general"? It's a general formula for 2x2, no enumeration. The hidden tests for n=2 use random matrices — the closed form handles all of them (including complex-dominant and degenerate-ish). Tolerance concern: np.allclose(A@v, λv) default: |A@v - λv| ≤ atol + rtol*|λv| elementwise. Residual magnitude ~ eps*|A|*|v| ~ 1e-15 * 3 * |v| vs tolerance 1e-5*|λv| ~ 1e-5*3*|v|. No problem.

Should I go closed form for 3x3, 4x4? Those get complicated; LAPACK is fast enough. Just 2x2.

OK, let me also reconsider the copy: with A C-order 10x10, copyto into the F-order buffer. Measure: np.copyto(_a[:n,:n], A). Alternative: A.T (a view, F-order, no copy!) — wait!! If A is C-contiguous, A.T is Fortran-contiguous and shares memory! But the data layout: A.T's memory is the same as A's memory (C-order) — I need an F-order array holding column-major data of A... no, wait. Let me think again.

LAPACK expects a pointer to memory where a[i,j] (i=row, j=col) is at offset j*lda + i (column-major). If I hand it A's C-order memory with lda=n: it reads a[row,col] = mem[col*n + row]... no, wrong: LAPACK's "element (i,j)" (1-based, i=row index in the math sense) is at mem[j*lda+i-1]. For C-order A, mem[i*n+j] = A[i][j]. LAPACK's mem[j*n+i] = A[j][i] = A^T[i][j]... so LAPACK sees the matrix A^T (in the 0-based sense: the matrix whose (i,j) entry is A[j][i]). That's A^T. So passing C-order A directly computes the eigen-decomposition of A^T. Eigenvalues: same as A. Right eigenvectors of A^T = left eigenvectors of A. Not what I want.

However: could I compute the eigen-decomposition of A^T and then... I need a right eigenvector of A. Is there a relation? For a non-symmetric A, in general there's no cheap relation. So no.

But wait: what if the evaluator's A is... no, np.random.normal → C-order. So copy/transpose is required. ~2-3µs. OK.

Faster copy alternatives:
- np.copyto(dst, src): ufunc machinery ~2µs.
- _a[:n,:n][:, :n] = A → slice + setitem, similar.
- Write the column copy manually? _a[:, :n] = A.T... hmm A.T is a view (F-contiguous, no memory copy), and the assignment does the transpose copy in C: _a[:n,:n][:,?]... assignment to an F-order slice from an F-order source: numpy detects both are contiguous and does a fast block copy? For _a[:n,:n] (F-order, shape (n,n), contiguous in Fortran order) and A.T (F-order, same shape): numpy might do a simple memcpy of the whole block!! Because "F-order block copy": both arrays are contiguous in memory with the same layout (each column contiguous) — but the column strides are both n (lda=n)... in-memory A.T: A.T's column j = A's row j = A.mem[j*n : j*n+n] — contiguous block of n doubles. My _a's column j: _a.mem[j*n : j*n+n]. Same layout! So the copy = a memcpy of n*n doubles. numpy might take the fast path. Let me measure np.copyto vs slice-assignment vs a direct memoryview trick.

Actually the simplest and fastest: use memoryview: mv_dst = memoryview(_a); mv_src = memoryview(A.T)? memoryview on non-byte arrays: 2D format 'd'... memoryview copying: mv = memoryview(A.T).cast... hmm, memoryview of a 2D float array has shape (n,n), format 'd'. Assigning mv_dst[:] = mv_src[:] for two 2D memoryviews with the same format does a strided copy per element (in C, fast ~n² iterations, no numpy overhead!). n²=100 → ~0.3µs. Might be the fastest. Let me test: memoryview assignment for 2D with identical shape/format does memcpy if both are contiguous in C... A.T is F-contiguous, not C-contiguous. Python's memoryview copy logic: if dst and src have the same format and shape, copy per element via strided copy... I think CPython's buffer copy handles contiguous C-layout specially; F-layout goes the strided path: 100 elements × ~2ns = 0.2µs.

Looking at v2's code:
```python
    if not isinstance(A, np.ndarray):
        A = np.asarray(A)
    n = A.shape[0]
    if (A.ndim != 2 or A.shape[1] != n or n < 2 or n > _MAXN
            or not np.issubdtype(A.dtype, np.floating)):
        return _fallback(A)
    arr = A if A.dtype is np.dtype(np.float64) else np.ascontiguousarray(A, dtype
=np.float64)
    if n == _MAXN:
        np.copyto(_a, arr, casting="same_kind")
    else:
        np.copyto(_a[:n, :n], arr, casting="same_kind")
```

n=10 → `np.copyto(_a, arr, casting="same_kind")` — the full buffer copy! Not the sliced `np
.copyto(_a[:n, :n], A)`. copyto of a full 10x10 with casting="same_kind"… should still be about 
1µs.
